Municipalities, as the states, have autonomous administrations, collect their own taxes and receive a share of taxes collected by the federal and state government.[^cite_note-Constituição-21] Each has an elected mayor and legislative body, but no separate Court of Law. Indeed, a Court of Law organized by the state can encompass many municipalities in a single justice administrative division called *[comarca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comarca)*.[^cite_note-Constituição-21]

Brazil's constitution also provides for the creation of [federal territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_territories_of_Brazil), which are administrative divisions directly controlled by the federal government. However, there are currently no federal territories in the country, as the 1988 constitution abolished the last three: Amapá and Roraima (which gained statehood) and Fernando de Noronha, which became a state district of [Pernambuco](229525.md).[^cite_note-245][^cite_note-246]

### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Brazil)



[Itamaraty Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itamaraty_Palace), the seat of the [Ministry of Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs_\(Brazil\))

Brazil's international relations are based on Article 4 of the Federal Constitution, which establishes [non-intervention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-interventionism), [self-determination](29269.md), [international cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internationalism_\(politics\)) and the [peaceful settlement of conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peacebuilding) as the guiding principles of Brazil's relationship with other countries and multilateral organizations.[^cite_note-247] According to the constitution, the president has ultimate authority over foreign policy, while Congress is tasked with reviewing and considering all diplomatic nominations and [international treaties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty), as well as legislation relating to Brazilian foreign policy.[^cite_note-248]

Brazil's foreign policy is a by-product of the country's position as a [regional power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_power) in Latin America, a leader among [developing countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developing_countries), and an emerging [world power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_power).[^cite_note-249] Brazilian foreign policy has generally been based on the principles of [multilateralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multilateralism), peaceful dispute settlement, and non-intervention in the affairs of other countries.[^cite_note-250] Brazil is a founding member state of the [Community of Portuguese Language Countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_of_Portuguese_Language_Countries) (CPLP), also known as the Lusophone Commonwealth, an international organization and political association of [Lusophone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lusophone) nations.

An increasingly well-developed tool of Brazil's foreign policy is providing aid as a donor to other developing countries.[^cite_note-ODI1-251] Brazil does not just use its growing economic strength to provide financial aid, but it also provides high levels of expertise and most importantly of all, a quiet non-confrontational diplomacy to improve governance levels.[^cite_note-ODI1-251] Total aid is estimated to be around \$1 billion per year.[^cite_note-ODI1-251] In addition, Brazil already managed a [peacekeeping mission in Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Stabilisation_Mission_in_Haiti) (\$350 million) and makes in-kind contributions to the [World Food Programme](33582.md) (\$300 million).[^cite_note-ODI1-251] The scale of this aid places it on par with China and India.[^cite_note-ODI1-251] The Brazilian [South-South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South-South) aid has been described as a "global model in waiting".[^cite_note-252]

### Military

Main article: [Brazilian Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Armed_Forces)



Brazilian Navy's flagship [PHM *Atlântico*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_helicopter_carrier_Atl%C3%A2ntico) and [frigate *Liberal* (F-43)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_frigate_Liberal_\(F-43\)) (background)

The armed forces of Brazil are the [largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_number_of_military_and_paramilitary_personnel) in Latin America by active personnel and the largest in terms of military equipment.[^cite_note-253] It consists of the [Brazilian Army](1047559.md) (including the [Army Aviation Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Army_Aviation)), the [Brazilian Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Navy) (including the [Marine Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Marine_Corps) and [Naval Aviation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Naval_Aviation)) and the [Brazilian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Air_Force). Brazil's [conscription policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscription_in_Brazil) gives it one of the world's largest military forces, estimated at more than 1.6 million [reservists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Reserve) annually.[^cite_note-254] The Air Force is the largest in Latin America and has about 700 crewed aircraft in service and effective about 67,000 personnel.[^cite_note-255]

Numbering close to 236,000 active personnel,[^cite_note-256] the Brazilian Army has the largest number of armored vehicles in South America, including armored transports and [tanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_tank).[^cite_note-militarypower1-257] The states' [Military Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Police_\(Brazil\)) and the [Military Firefighters Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Firefighters_Corps) are designated as auxiliary forces of the army by the constitution, but are under the control of each state's governor.[^cite_note-Constituição-21]

Brazil's navy once operated some of the most powerful warships in the world with the two [*Minas Geraes*-class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minas_Geraes-class_battleship) [dreadnoughts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dreadnought), sparking a [naval arms race between Argentina, Brazil, and Chile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_American_dreadnought_race).[^cite_note-258] Today, it is a [green water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green-water_navy) force and has a group of specialized elite in retaking ships and naval facilities, [GRUMEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GRUMEC), unit specially trained to protect Brazilian oil platforms along its coast.[^cite_note-259] As of 2022, it is the only navy in Latin America that operates a [helicopter carrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helicopter_carrier), [NAM *Atlântico*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_aircraft_carrier_Atl%C3%A2ntico) and one of twelve navies in the world to operate or have one under construction.[^cite_note-Poder_Naval-260]

### Law enforcement and crime

Main articles: [Law enforcement in Brazil](4876365.md) and [Crime in Brazil](15348345.md)



Headquarters of the Federal Police of Brazil in Brasília

In Brazil, the constitution establishes six different police agencies for law enforcement: [Federal Police Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Police_Department), [Federal Highway Police](14512223.md), [Federal Railroad Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Railroad_Police), Federal, District and State Penal Police (included by the Constitutional Amendment No. 104, of 2019), [Military Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Police_\(Brazil\)) and [Civil Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Police_\(Brazil\)). Of these, the former three are affiliated with federal authorities, the latter two are subordinate to state governments and the Penal Police can be subordinated to the federal or state/district government. All police forces are overseen by the executive branch of the federal or state governments.[^cite_note-Constituição-21] The [National Public Security Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Public_Security_Force) also can act in public disorder situations arising anywhere in the country.[^cite_note-261]

The country has high levels of violent crime, such as gun violence and homicides. In 2022, the [United Nations Office on Drugs and Crime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Office_on_Drugs_and_Crime) (UNODC) estimated an intentional homicide rate of 21.1 per 100,000 inhabitants.[^cite_note-262] The number considered acceptable by the [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO) is about 10 homicides per 100,000 inhabitants.[^cite_note-263] In 2025, Brazil recorded 34,086 homicides, down from 38,374 in 2024,[^cite_note-\:0-264] and from a record 63,880 in 2017.[^cite_note-265] Homicide rates vary regionally. While in [São Paulo](229422.md) the homicide rate registered in 2025 was 5.4 per 100,000 inhabitants, in [Ceará](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cear%C3%A1) it was 32.6 per 100,000 inhabitants.[^cite_note-\:0-264] The national homicide rate for 2025 was 16 per 100,000 inhabitants, the lowest in over a decade.[^cite_note-\:0-264]

Brazil also has high levels of incarceration. It had the third largest prison population in the world of approximately 909,067 prisoners in 2024, which put it only behind the United States (1,808,100) and China (1,690,000).[^cite_note-266] The high number of prisoners eventually overloaded the Brazilian prison system, leading to a shortfall of about 200,000 accommodations.[^cite_note-267]

### Human rights

See also: [Human rights in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Brazil)

[Human rights in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Brazil) include the [right to life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_life) and [freedom of speech](21401843.md); and condemnation of slavery and torture. The nation ratified the [American Convention on Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Convention_on_Human_Rights).[^cite_note-268] The 2017 [Freedom in the World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_in_the_World) report by [Freedom House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House) gives Brazil a score of "2" for both political rights and civil liberties; "1" represents the most free, and "7", the least.[^cite_note-269] According to [UNESCO](21786641.md), "Brazil promotes a vast array of actions for the advancement and defense of human rights, even though it faces enormous social and economic inequalities".[^cite_note-270] Same-sex couples in Brazil have held nationwide [marriage rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_Brazil) since May 2013.[^cite_note-edition.cnn.com-271]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Brazil](3634.md)

See also: [Brazilian real](34201323.md), [Agriculture in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Brazil), [Mining in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mining_in_Brazil), and [Industry in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industry_in_Brazil)



[São Paulo](390875.md) is considered the main financial center of Brazil.



B3, the largest stock exchange of Latin America by market capitalization

[^cite_note-272]



Soybean crop in [Tangará da Serra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tangar%C3%A1_da_Serra), [Mato Grosso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mato_Grosso)

[^cite_note-exportaçãoagrícola-273]



The [C-390](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embraer_C-390_Millennium), developed by [Embraer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embraer), the third largest producer of civil aircraft, after [Boeing](18933266.md) and [Airbus](26220236.md)

[^cite_note-274]

Brazil is a [developing country](78449.md) with an [upper-middle income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GNI_\(nominal\)_per_capita#Upper-middle_income_group) [mixed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_economy) [market economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_economy) that is rich in natural resources.[^cite_note-275] It has the largest national economy in Latin America, the [tenth largest economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) in the world by nominal GDP, and the [eighth largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)) by [PPP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity).[^cite_note-276] After rapid growth in preceding decades, Brazil [entered a recession in 2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014%E2%80%932016_Brazilian_economic_recession) amid a political corruption scandal and nationwide protests; in 2024, the economy began showing consistent significant growth.[^cite_note-277] Brazil has a [labor force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labor_force) of roughly 100 million, which is the world's [fifth largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_labour_force).[^cite_note-278] Its [foreign exchange reserves](1015869.md) are the [tenth-highest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_foreign_exchange_reserves) in the world.[^cite_note-279] The [B3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B3_\(stock_exchange\)) in São Paulo is the [largest stock exchange of Latin America by market capitalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_stock_exchanges). Roughly one-fifth of Brazilians live in poverty: about 3.8% of the total population lives at \$3.00 a day,[^cite_note-280] while about 23% live at \$8.30 a day.[^cite_note-281] Brazil's economy suffers from [endemic corruption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_in_Brazil) and [high income inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_inequality_in_Brazil).[^cite_note-282] The [Brazilian real](34201323.md) is the national currency.

Brazil's diversified economy includes agriculture, industry and a wide range of services.[^cite_note-BansalPhatak2009-283] The large service sector accounts for about 72.7% of total GDP, followed by the industrial sector (20.7%), while the [agriculture sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Brazil) is by far the smallest, making up 6.6% of total GDP.[^cite_note-284]

Brazil is one of the [largest producers of various agricultural commodities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_producing_countries_of_agricultural_commodities),[^cite_note-285] and also has a large [cooperative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooperative) sector that provides 50% of the food in the country.[^cite_note-286] It has been the world's largest [producer of coffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coffee_production_in_Brazil) for the last 150 years[^cite_note-Neilson102-33] and is the world's largest producer of sugarcane, soy, coffee and oranges; is one of the top five producers of maize, cotton, lemon, tobacco, pineapple, banana, beans, coconut, watermelon and papaya; and is one of the top 10 world producers of cocoa, cashew, mango, rice, tomato, sorghum, tangerine, avocado, persimmon, and guava, among others. Regarding livestock, it is one of the five largest producers of chicken meat, beef, pork and cow's milk in the world.[^cite_note-287]

In the [mining sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mining_in_Brazil), Brazil is among the largest producers of iron ore, copper, gold,[^cite_note-288] bauxite, manganese, tin, [niobium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niobium),[^cite_note-289] and nickel. In terms of precious stones, Brazil is the world's largest producer of [amethyst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amethyst), [topaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topaz), [agate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agate) and one of the main producers of [tourmaline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourmaline), [emerald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emerald), [aquamarine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aquamarine_\(gemstone\)), [garnet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garnet) and [opal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opal).[^cite_note-290][^cite_note-291] The country is a major exporter of soy, iron ore, pulp (cellulose), maize, beef, chicken meat, soybean meal, sugar, coffee, tobacco, cotton, orange juice, footwear, airplanes, cars, vehicle parts, gold, ethanol and semi-finished iron, among other products.[^cite_note-292][^cite_note-293]

Brazil is the world's [24th-largest exporter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_exports) and [26th-largest importer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_imports) as of 2021.[^cite_note-294][^cite_note-295] China is its largest trading partner, accounting for 32% of the total trade. Other large trading partners include the United States, Argentina, the Netherlands and Canada.[^cite_note-296] Its [automotive industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automotive_industry_in_Brazil) is the [eighth-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_motor_vehicle_production) in the world.[^cite_note-297] In the [food industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_industry), Brazil was the second-largest exporter of processed foods in the world in 2019.[^cite_note-298] The country was the second-largest producer of [pulp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulp_\(paper\)) in the world and the eighth-largest producer of paper in 2016.[^cite_note-299] In the [footwear industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Footwear_industry), Brazil was the fourth-largest producer in 2019.[^cite_note-300] It was also the ninth-largest producer of steel in the world.[^cite_note-301][^cite_note-302][^cite_note-303] In 2018, the [chemical industry](58721.md) of Brazil was the eighth-largest in the world.[^cite_note-304][^cite_note-305][^cite_note-306] Although it was among the five largest world producers in 2013, Brazil's textile industry is very little integrated into world trade.[^cite_note-307]

The tertiary sector (trade and services) represented 75.8% of the country's GDP in 2018, according to the IBGE. The service sector was responsible for 60% of GDP and trade for 13%. It covers commerce, transport, education, social and health services, research and development, sports activities, etc.[^cite_note-308][^cite_note-309] Micro and small businesses represent 30% of the country's GDP. In the commercial sector they represent 53% of the GDP within the activities of the sector.[^cite_note-310]

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Brazil](8794264.md)



&#x20;[Iguaçu National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igua%C3%A7u_National_Park) in [Paraná](229411.md)

Tourism in Brazil is a growing sector and key to the economies of several regions of the country. The country had 6.36 million visitors in 2015, ranking in terms of the international tourist arrivals as the main destination in South America and second in Latin America after Mexico.[^cite_note-311] Revenues from international tourists reached US\$6 billion in 2010, showing a recovery from the [2008–2009 economic crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late-2000s_recession).[^cite_note-UNWTO2011-312] Historical records of 5.4 million visitors and US\$6.8 billion in receipts were reached in 2011.[^cite_note-MT1_2011-313][^cite_note-MT2_2011-314] In the list of world tourist destinations, in 2018, Brazil was the 48th most visited country, with 6.6 million tourists (and revenues of 5.9 billion dollars).[^cite_note-315]

Natural areas are its most popular tourism product, a combination of [ecotourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecotourism) with leisure and recreation, mainly sun and beach, and adventure travel, as well as cultural tourism. Among the most popular destinations are the Amazon Rainforest, beaches and dunes in the [Northeast Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Region,_Brazil), the [Pantanal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pantanal) in the [Center-West Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center-West_Region,_Brazil), beaches at [Rio de Janeiro](229419.md) and [Santa Catarina](256305.md), cultural tourism in [Minas Gerais](222651.md) and business trips to [São Paulo](390875.md).[^cite_note-Palhares2012-316]

In terms of the 2024 [Travel and Tourism Competitiveness Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travel_and_Tourism_Development_Index) (TTCI), which is a measurement of the factors that make it attractive to develop business in the travel and tourism industry of individual countries, Brazil ranked in the 26th place at the world's level, third in the Americas, after Canada and United States.[^cite_note-317] [Domestic tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_tourism) is a key market segment for the tourism industry in Brazil. In 2005, 51 million Brazilian nationals made ten times more trips than foreign tourists and spent five times more money than their international counterparts.[^cite_note-FIPE007-318] The main destination states in 2023 were [São Paulo](229422.md), [Rio de Janeiro](229419.md), and [Rio Grande do Sul](256268.md).[^cite_note-319][^cite_note-320] The main source of tourists for the entire country is São Paulo state.[^cite_note-321] In terms of tourism revenues, the top earners by state were [São Paulo](229422.md) and [Bahia](199070.md).[^cite_note-322] For 2005, the three main trip purposes were visiting friends and family (53.1%), sun and beach (40.8%), and cultural tourism (12.5%).[^cite_note-FIPE006-323]

### Science and technology

Main article: [Science and technology in Brazil](1525228.md)



[VLS-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VLS-1) at the [Alcântara Launch Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alc%C3%A2ntara_Launch_Center) of the Brazilian Space Agency, in [Alcântara, Maranhão](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alc%C3%A2ntara,_Maranh%C3%A3o)



[Sirius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sirius_\(synchrotron_light_source\)), a [diffraction-limited storage ring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diffraction-limited_storage_ring) [synchrotron light source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synchrotron_light_source) at the [Laboratório Nacional de Luz Síncrotron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laborat%C3%B3rio_Nacional_de_Luz_S%C3%ADncrotron), in [Campinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campinas), São Paulo

Technological research in Brazil is largely carried out in public universities and research institutes, with the majority of funding for basic research coming from various government agencies.[^cite_note-DevelopmentStaff2006v-324] Brazil's most esteemed technological hubs are the [Oswaldo Cruz Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funda%C3%A7%C3%A3o_Oswaldo_Cruz), the [Butantan Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Butantan), the Air Force's [Aerospace Technical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_General_Command_for_Aerospace_Technology), the [Brazilian Agricultural Research Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empresa_Brasileira_de_Pesquisa_Agropecu%C3%A1ria) and the [National Institute for Space Research](30875637.md).[^cite_note-Scientific2010f-325][^cite_note-HarveySmid2010-326]

The [Brazilian Space Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Space_Agency) has the most developed [space program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lists_of_space_programs) in Latin America, with significant capabilities in launch vehicles, launch sites, and satellite manufacturing.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrocittiVallance2012\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidvP9jHaoL_s4CpgPA628_628\]-327] Brazil is the only country in the Southern Hemisphere involved in the [International Space Station](15043.md) (ISS).[^cite_note-NASA-328] The country also [develops submarines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Submarine_Development_Program) and is the world's third largest aircraft producer, primarily through [Embraer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embraer).

The country is also a pioneer in the search for oil in deep water, from where it extracts 73% of its reserves. Uranium is enriched at the [Resende Nuclear Fuel Factory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resende_Nuclear_Fuel_Factory), mostly for research purposes (as Brazil obtains 88% of its electricity from [hydroelectricity](381399.md)),[^cite_note-329] and the country's [first nuclear submarine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_submarine_%C3%81lvaro_Alberto) is expected to launch in 2029.[^cite_note-330]

Brazil is one of the three countries in Latin America[^cite_note-331] with an operational [Synchrotron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synchrotron) Laboratory (a research facility on physics, chemistry, material science and life sciences), and the only Latin American country with a domestic [fabrication plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semiconductor_fabrication_plant), the [CEITEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CEITEC).[^cite_note-332] According to the Global Information Technology Report 2009–2010 of the World Economic Forum, Brazil is the world's 61st largest developer of information technology.[^cite_note-333] Brazil ranked 52nd in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index) in 2025, up from 66th in 2019.[^cite_note-334][^cite_note-335][^cite_note-336]

Among the most renowned Brazilian inventors are priests [Bartolomeu de Gusmão](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bartolomeu_de_Gusm%C3%A3o), [Landell de Moura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landell_de_Moura) and Francisco João de Azevedo, besides [Alberto Santos-Dumont](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberto_Santos-Dumont),[^cite_note-337] [Evaristo Conrado Engelberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evaristo_Conrado_Engelberg),[^cite_note-patentbritish-338] [Manuel Dias de Abreu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Dias_de_Abreu),[^cite_note-339] [Andreas Pavel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andreas_Pavel)[^cite_note-340] and Nélio José Nicolai.[^cite_note-341] Brazilian science is represented by the likes of [César Lattes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%A9sar_Lattes) (Brazilian physicist Pathfinder of *[Pi Meson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pion)*),[^cite_note-Lattes-342] [Mário Schenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A1rio_Schenberg) (considered the greatest theoretical physicist of Brazil),[^cite_note-343] [José Leite Lopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Leite_Lopes) (the only Brazilian physicist holder of the *UNESCO Science Prize*),[^cite_note-344] [Artur Avila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artur_Avila) (the first Latin American winner of the [Fields Medal](10859.md)),[^cite_note-345] and [Fritz Müller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fritz_M%C3%BCller) (pioneer in factual support of the theory of evolution by [Charles Darwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Darwin)).[^cite_note-West,_David_A_2003-346]

### Energy

Main article: [Energy in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Brazil)



The [Itaipu Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itaipu_Dam) on the [Paraná River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paran%C3%A1_River), the second largest of the world. [Brazilian energy matrix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_policy_of_Brazil) is one of the [cleanest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clean_energy) in the world.



[Wind farm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_farm) in [Parnaíba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parna%C3%ADba), [Piauí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piau%C3%AD). Brazil is one of the 5 largest producers of wind energy in the world.

Brazil is the world's [eleventh-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_energy_consumption_and_production) energy consumer.[^cite_note-347] Much of its energy comes from [renewable sources](25784.md), particularly [hydroelectricity](381399.md) and [ethanol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethanol); the [Itaipu Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itaipu_Dam) is the world's largest [hydroelectric plant](381399.md) by energy generation,[^cite_note-SchmidtOnyango2011-348] and the country has other large plants such as [Belo Monte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belo_Monte_Dam) and [Tucuruí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tucuru%C3%AD_Dam). The first car with an ethanol engine was produced in 1978 and the first airplane engine running on ethanol in 2005.[^cite_note-OECDDevelopment2001-349]

At the end of 2021, Brazil ranked second in the world by installed [hydroelectric power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroelectric_power) (109.4 GW) and [biomass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biomass) (15.8 GW), seventh in installed [wind power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_power) (21.1 GW) and 14th in installed [solar power](13690575.md) (13.0 GW)—on track to also become one of the top 10 in the world in solar energy.[^cite_note-350] At the end of 2024, Brazil was the fourth largest producer of wind energy in the world (107.8 TWh), behind only China, the United States and Germany, and the world's fifth largest producer of solar energy (74.7 TWh). Overall, Brazil [ranks third globally in renewable electricity production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_renewable_electricity_production), after China and the United States, accounting for 7% of the global total.[^cite_note-ember-351]

The Brazilian [energy matrix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_materials) is characterized by a higher degree of renewable energy than the global average; in 2022, the world energy matrix was only 14% renewable energy, compared to 45% in Brazil. Petroleum and oil products made up 34.3% of the country's matrix; sugar cane derivatives, 18%; hydraulic energy, 12.4%; natural gas, 12.2%; firewood and charcoal, 8.8%; varied renewable energies, 7%; mineral coal, 5.3%; nuclear, 1.4%, and other non-renewable energies, 0.6%.[^cite_note-Matriz_Energética_e_Elétrica-352] Similarly, the vast majority of Brazilian electricity is derived from renewable sources: 83% compared to 25% worldwide. The leading sources are hydraulic energy at 64.9%; biomass, 8.4%; wind, 8.6%; solar, 1%; natural gas, 9.3%; oil products, 2%; nuclear, 2.5%; and coal and derivatives, 3.3%.[^cite_note-Matriz_Energética_e_Elétrica-352] Brazil has the largest electricity sector in Latin America; its capacity at the end of 2021 was 181,532 MW.[^cite_note-Ministério_de_Minas_e_Energia-353]

The Brazilian government has embarked on a program over the decades to reduce dependence on imported oil, which previously accounted for more than 70% of the country's oil needs. Brazil became self-sufficient in oil in 2006–2007. By the end of 2021, the country was the seventh largest oil producer in the world, with an average of nearly three million barrels per day, and became an exporter of the product.[^cite_note-354][^cite_note-355]

### Transportation

Main article: [Transport in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Brazil)



Terminal 3 of the [São Paulo–Guarulhos International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Paulo%E2%80%93Guarulhos_International_Airport), the [busiest airport in Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_busiest_airports_in_Latin_America)



[BR-116](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BR-116) in [São José dos Campos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Jos%C3%A9_dos_Campos), [São Paulo](229422.md), the longest [highway](1299268.md) in the country,

[^cite_note-356]

&#x20;with 4,542 km (2,822 mi) of extension

[^cite_note-Extensão-357]

Brazilian roads are the primary carriers of freight and passenger traffic. The [road system](1299268.md) totaled 2,000,000 km (1,242,742 mi) in 2023,[^cite_note-358] ranking [fourth largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_road_network_size) by size globally. The total of paved roads increased from 35,496 km (22,056 mi) in 1967 to 215,000 km (133,595 mi) in 2018.[^cite_note-359]

Brazil's [railway system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transport_in_Brazil) has been declining since 1945, when emphasis shifted to highway construction. The country's total railway track length was 30,576 km (18,999 mi) in 2015,[^cite_note-360] as compared with 31,848 km (19,789 mi) in 1970, making it the [ninth largest network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_rail_transport_network_size) in the world. Most of the railway system belonged to the [Federal Railroad Network Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RFFSA) (RFFSA), which was privatized in 2007.[^cite_note-361] The [São Paulo Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Paulo_Metro) began operating on 14 September 1974 as the first underground transit system in Brazil.[^cite_note-362]

There are over 4,900 airports and airfields in Brazil, second only to the United States.[^cite_note-\:1-19] [São Paulo–Guarulhos International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Paulo%E2%80%93Guarulhos_International_Airport), near São Paulo, is the largest and busiest airport with nearly 43 million passengers annually, while handling the vast majority of commercial traffic for the country.[^cite_note-Palhares2012x-363][^cite_note-364]

For freight transport, waterways are of importance. The [industrial zones of Manaus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Economic_Zone_of_Manaus) can be reached only by means of the Solimões–Amazonas waterway (3,250 kilometers or 2,020 miles in length, with a minimum depth of six meters or 20 feet). The country also has 50,000 kilometers (31,000 miles) of waterways.[^cite_note-2012ciagini-365] Coastal shipping links widely separated parts of the country. Bolivia and Paraguay have been given free ports at [Santos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santos,_S%C3%A3o_Paulo). Of the 36 deep-water ports, [Santos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Santos), [Itajaí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Itaja%C3%AD), [Rio Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Rio_Grande), [Paranaguá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Paranagu%C3%A1), [Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Rio_de_Janeiro), Sepetiba, [Vitória](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Tubar%C3%A3o), [Suape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suape_Port), [Manaus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Manaus) and [São Francisco do Sul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_S%C3%A3o_Francisco_do_Sul) are the most important.[^cite_note-366] Bulk carriers have to wait up to 18 days before being serviced; container ships take 36.3 hours on average.[^cite_note-367]

## Demographics

Main articles: [Demographics of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Brazil) and [Brazilians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilians)

See also: [Immigration to Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_Brazil) and [List of Brazilian states by population density](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Brazilian_states_by_population_density)



Population density of Brazilian municipalities

According to the latest official projection, Brazil's estimated population was 214,211,951 on 1 July 2026—an increase from the figure of 203 million reported by the 2022 census.[^cite_note-368][^cite_note-369] The population of Brazil, as recorded by the 2008 PNAD, was approximately 190 million (22.31 inhabitants per square kilometer or 57.8/sq mi), with a ratio of men to women of 0.95\:1 and 83.75% of the population defined as urban.[^cite_note-ibge2008pnad-370] The population is heavily concentrated in the Southeast (89 million inhabitants) and Northeast (54.6 million inhabitants), while the two most extensive regions, the Center-West and the North, which together make up 64.12% of Brazilian territory, have a total of only 33.8 million inhabitants.

The first census in Brazil was carried out [in 1872](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1872_Brazilian_census) and recorded a population of 9,930,478.[^cite_note-371] From 1880 to 1930, four million Europeans arrived.[^cite_note-372] Brazil's population increased significantly between 1940 and 1970, because of a decline in the [mortality rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mortality_rate), even though the [birth rate](515898.md) underwent a slight decline. In the 1940s the annual population growth rate was 2.4%, rising to 3.0% in the 1950s and remaining at 2.9% in the 1960s, as life expectancy rose from 44 to 54 years,[^cite_note-373] and to 72.6 years in 2007.[^cite_note-374] It has been steadily falling since the 1960s, from 3.04% per year between 1950 and 1960 to 1.05% in 2008 and is expected to fall to a negative value of –0.29% by 2050,[^cite_note-375] thus completing the [demographic transition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographic_transition).[^cite_note-376] In 2022, the illiteracy rate was roughly 7%,[^cite_note-RF-377] a significant decline from 11.48% in 2008.[^cite_note-378] By comparison, in 1940, more than half the population (54%) was illiterate.[^cite_note-RF-377]

### Urbanization

Main article: [List of cities in Brazil by population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Brazil_by_population)

According to IBGE (Brazilian Institute of Geography and Statistics) urban areas already concentrate 84.35% of the population, while the Southeast region remains the most populated one, with over 80 million inhabitants.[^cite_note-379] The largest urban agglomerations in Brazil are [São Paulo](390875.md), [Rio de Janeiro](25936.md), and [Belo Horizonte](82198.md)—all in the Southeastern Region—with 21.1, 12.3, and 5.1 million inhabitants respectively.[^cite_note-concentrações_urbanas-380][^cite_note-381][^cite_note-382] The majority of state capitals are the largest cities in their states, except for [Vitória](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vit%C3%B3ria,_Brazil), the capital of [Espírito Santo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esp%C3%ADrito_Santo), and [Florianópolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florian%C3%B3polis), the capital of [Santa Catarina](256305.md).[^cite_note-383]
