### Race and ethnicity

Main article: [Race and ethnicity in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_and_ethnicity_in_Brazil)

Racial groups in Brazil ([2022 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Brazilian_census))

[^cite_note-census2022-5]

1. [Pardos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pardo_Brazilians) ([mixed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed-race_Brazilian)) (45.3%)

2. [Whites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Brazilians) (43.5%)

3. [Blacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Brazilians) (10.2%)

4. [Indigenous](55510.md) (0.60%)

5. [East Asians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Brazilians) (0.42%)

According to the [2022 Brazilian census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Brazilian_census), 45.3% of the population (92.1 million) described themselves as [Pardo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pardo_Brazilians) (meaning [brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_\(racial_classification\)) or multiracial), 43.5% (88.2 million) as [White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Brazilians), 10.2% (20.7 million) as [Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Brazilians), 0.6% (1.2 million) as [Indigenous](55510.md) and 0.4% (850,000) as [East Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Brazilians) (officially called *[yellow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongoloid)* or *amarela*).[^cite_note-PNADIBGE-385]

Since the arrival of the Portuguese in 1500, considerable genetic mixing between Amerindians, Europeans, and Africans has taken place in all regions of the country:

- European ancestry being dominant according to all autosomal studies undertaken covering the population, accounting for between 60% and 65% of the average genetic makeup of the Brazilian population.

  [^cite_note-alvaro.com.br2-386]

  [^cite_note-www1.folha.uol.com.br2-387]

  [^cite_note-bdtd.bce.unb.br2-388]

  [^cite_note-plosone.org2-389]

- African ancestry among the Brazilians is estimated at 20% to 25% of the average genetic makeup

  [^cite_note-bdtd.bce.unb.br2-388]

  [^cite_note-wiley12-390]

  [^cite_note-scielo.br2-391]

- Indigenous ancestry is significant and present in all regions of Brazil, accounting for around 15% to 20% of the average genetic ancestry of Brazilians.

  [^cite_note-wiley12-390]

  [^cite_note-alvaro.com.br2-386]

  [^cite_note-bdtd.bce.unb.br2-388]

  [^cite_note-plosone.org2-389]

  [^cite_note-pmid145958742-392]

  [^cite_note-393]

From the 19th century, Brazil opened its borders to [immigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_Brazil). About five million people from over 60 countries migrated to Brazil between 1808 and 1972, most of them of [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Brazilian), [Italian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Brazilian), [Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilians_of_Spanish_descent), [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Brazilian), [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Brazilian), [Ukrainian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Brazilian), [Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Brazilian), [Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Brazilian), [African](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Brazilian), [Armenian](387816.md), [Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russians_in_Brazil), [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Brazilian), [Japanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_Brazilian), [Korean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Brazilian) and [Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Brazilian) origin.[^cite_note-394][^cite_note-395][^cite_note-396] Brazil has the second-largest Jewish community in Latin America after Argentina making up 0.06% of its population.[^cite_note-397] Outside of the [Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world), Brazil also has the largest [population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_diaspora) of Arab ancestry in the world, with 15–20 million people.[^cite_note-Ferabolli2014-398][^cite_note-Amar2014-399] According to Brazil's Ministry of Foreign Affairs, Brazil is home to a Lebanese diaspora of 7-10 million, surpassing the population of Lebanese individuals residing in Lebanon.[^cite_note-400]

Brazilian society is more [markedly divided by social class lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_issues_in_Brazil), although a high [income disparity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_inequality_in_Brazil) is found [between race groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_apartheid_in_Brazil), so racism and [classism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class_discrimination) often overlap. The brown population (officially called *pardo* in Portuguese)[^cite_note-Coelho_1996,_p._268-401][^cite_note-Vesentini_1988,_p._117-402] is a broad category that includes *[caboclos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caboclo)* (assimilated Amerindians in general, and descendants of Whites and Natives), *[mulatos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mulatto)* (descendants of primarily Whites and Afro-Brazilians) and *[cafuzos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zambo)* (descendants of Afro-Brazilians and Natives).[^cite_note-Coelho_1996,_p._268-401][^cite_note-Vesentini_1988,_p._117-402][^cite_note-403][^cite_note-404][^cite_note-Moreira_1981,_p._108-405] Higher percents of Blacks, mulattoes and tri-racials can be found in the eastern coast of the Northeastern region from Bahia to Paraíba[^cite_note-Moreira_1981,_p._108-405][^cite_note-406] and also in northern Maranhão,[^cite_note-407][^cite_note-408] southern Minas Gerais[^cite_note-Azevedo_1971,_p._161-409] and eastern Rio de Janeiro.[^cite_note-Moreira_1981,_p._108-405][^cite_note-Azevedo_1971,_p._161-409]

People of considerable Amerindian ancestry form the majority of the population in the Northern, Northeastern and Center-Western regions.[^cite_note-410] In 2007, the [National Indian Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funda%C3%A7%C3%A3o_Nacional_do_%C3%8Dndio) estimated that Brazil has 67 different uncontacted tribes, up from their estimate of 40 in 2005. Brazil is believed to have the largest number of [uncontacted peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncontacted_peoples) in the world.[^cite_note-411]

### Language

Main articles: [Languages of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Brazil), [Portuguese language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language), [Brazilian Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Portuguese), and [List of endangered languages in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_endangered_languages_in_Brazil)



[Alto Rio Negro Indigenous Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alto_Rio_Negro_Indigenous_Territory), in [São Gabriel da Cachoeira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Gabriel_da_Cachoeira), [Amazonas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amazonas_\(Brazilian_state\)), where [Nhengatu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nhengatu), [Tucano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tucano_language) and [Baniwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karu_language) are co-official languages



[Pomerode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pomerode), [Santa Catarina](256305.md), where the [East Pomeranian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Pomeranian_dialect) is the [second language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_language) (see [Brazilian German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_German))

The official language of Brazil is [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language) (Article 13 of the Constitution of the Federal Republic of Brazil), which almost all of the population speaks and is virtually the only language used in newspapers, radio, television, and for business and administrative purposes. Brazil is the only Portuguese-speaking nation in the Americas, making the language an important part of Brazilian national identity and giving it a national culture distinct from those of its Spanish-speaking neighbors.[^cite_note-language2-412]

[Brazilian Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Portuguese) has had its own development, mostly similar to 16th-century Central and Southern dialects of European Portuguese[^cite_note-413] (despite a very substantial number of Portuguese colonial settlers, and [more recent immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Brazilian), coming from [Northern regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Portugal), and in minor degree Portuguese [Macaronesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macaronesia)), with a few influences from the [Amerindian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_languages_of_the_Americas) and [African languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Africa), especially [West African](67393.md) and [Bantu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bantu_languages) restricted to the vocabulary only.[^cite_note-Portuguese-414] As a result, the language is somewhat different, mostly in phonology, from the language of Portugal and other Portuguese-speaking countries (the dialects of the other countries, partly because of the more recent end of Portuguese colonialism in these regions, have a closer connection to contemporary [European Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Portuguese)). These differences are comparable to those between [American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_English) and [British English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_English).[^cite_note-Portuguese-414]

The 2002 [sign language law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recognition_of_sign_languages)[^cite_note-Libras_2002-415] requires government authorities and public agencies to accept and provide information in *Língua Brasileira dos Sinais* or "LIBRAS", the [Brazilian Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Sign_Language), while a 2005 presidential edict[^cite_note-Libras_2005-416] extends this to require teaching of the language as a part of the education and [speech and language pathology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speech_and_language_pathology) curricula. LIBRAS teachers, instructors and translators are recognized professionals. Schools and health services must provide access ("[inclusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inclusion_\(education\))") to deaf people.[^cite_note-Russo2011-417]

Minority languages are spoken throughout the nation. One hundred and eighty Amerindian languages are spoken in remote areas and a significant number of other languages are spoken by immigrants and their descendants.[^cite_note-Portuguese-414] In the municipality of [São Gabriel da Cachoeira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Gabriel_da_Cachoeira), [Nheengatu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nheengatu_language) (a currently endangered [creole language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creole_language) that, together with its southern relative [língua geral paulista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%ADngua_geral_paulista), once was a major [lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca) in Brazil),[^cite_note-418] [Baniwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baniwa_of_I%C3%A7ana) and Tucano languages had been granted co-official status with Portuguese.[^cite_note-nyt-language-419]

There are significant communities of German (mostly the [Brazilian Hunsrückisch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riograndenser_Hunsr%C3%BCckisch), a High German language dialect) and Italian (mostly the [Talian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talian_dialect), a [Venetian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venetian_language) dialect) origins in the Southern and Southeastern regions, whose ancestors' native languages were carried along to Brazil, and which, still alive there, are influenced by the Portuguese language.[^cite_note-420][^cite_note-421] Talian is officially a historic heritage of [Rio Grande do Sul](256268.md),[^cite_note-422] and two German dialects possess co-official status in a few municipalities.[^cite_note-Stevenson1997-423] Italian is also recognized as "ethnic language" in [Santa Teresa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Teresa,_Esp%C3%ADrito_Santo) and [Vila Velha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vila_Velha), in the state of [Espírito Santo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esp%C3%ADrito_Santo).[^cite_note-424]

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Brazil)

Further information: [Catholic Church in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Brazil) and [Protestantism in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism_in_Brazil)

[Religion in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Brazil) (2022 census)

1. [Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Brazil) (56.8%)

2. [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism) (26.9%)

3. [No religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion) (9.28%)

4. [Spiritism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kardecist_spiritism) (1.84%)

5. Other religions (5.06%)

6. Not stated (0.17%)

Christianity is the country's predominant faith, with Catholicism being its largest denomination. Brazil has the [world's largest Catholic population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_by_country).[^cite_note-PEWCATHOLIC-425][^cite_note-USDS-426][^cite_note-427] According to the 2022 demographic census (the PNAD survey does not inquire about religion), 56.75% of the population followed Catholicism; 26.85% [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism_in_Brazil); 1.84% Kardecist spiritism; 5.06% other religions, undeclared or undetermined; while 9.28% had no religion.[^cite_note-census2022-5]

Religious diversity in Brazil developed from the meeting of the Catholic Church with the religious traditions of enslaved African peoples and indigenous peoples.[^cite_note-BoyleSheen2013-428] This confluence of faiths during the Portuguese colonization of Brazil led to the development of a diverse array of syncretistic practices within the overarching umbrella of Brazilian Catholic Church, characterized by traditional Portuguese festivities.[^cite_note-georgetown1-429]

Religious pluralism increased during the 20th century,[^cite_note-Morris2006a-430] and the Protestant community had grown to include over 22% of the population by 2010—partly due to a mixture of American missionary and [US government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_government) influence.[^cite_note-JeynesRobinson2012-431][^cite_note-432][^cite_note-433] The most common Protestant denominations are [Evangelical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelicalism) [Pentecostal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostalism) ones. Other Protestant branches with a notable presence in the country include the [Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptists), [Seventh-day Adventists](28632.md), [Lutherans](23371382.md) and the [Reformed tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calvinism).[^cite_note-434] In recent decades, Protestantism, particularly in forms of Pentecostalism and Evangelicalism, has spread in Brazil, while the proportion of Catholics had dropped significantly during the 2010s.[^cite_note-georgetown1-429][^cite_note-435] As they have spread throughout Brazil, many have even been deeply involved in Brazilian and international politics,[^cite_note-436][^cite_note-437] and Evangelical Protestant influence has been implicated in the [2022 Brazilian coup plot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Brazilian_coup_plot).[^cite_note-438] Since 2022, Evangelicals and Catholics have begun reconsidering religion as a political factor;[^cite_note-439] however, several Evangelical denominations have continued strategically expanding their power in national politics.[^cite_note-440]



The [Cristo Redentor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ_the_Redeemer_\(statue\)) statue towers over Rio de Janeiro.

After Protestantism, individuals professing no religion are also a significant group, having exceeded 8% of the population according to the 2010 census. The cities of [Boa Vista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boa_Vista,_Roraima), [Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador,_Bahia), and [Porto Velho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porto_Velho) have the greatest proportion of [Irreligious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion) residents in Brazil. [Teresina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teresina), [Fortaleza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortaleza), and [Florianópolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florian%C3%B3polis) were the most Roman Catholic in the country.[^cite_note-FGV_no_G1-441] [Greater Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Rio_de_Janeiro), not including the city proper, is the most irreligious and least Roman Catholic Brazilian periphery, while [Greater Porto Alegre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Porto_Alegre) and Greater [Fortaleza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortaleza) are on the opposite sides of the lists, respectively.[^cite_note-FGV_no_G1-441]

In October 2009, the Brazilian Senate approved, and enacted by the President of Brazil in February 2010, an agreement with the [Vatican](13393.md), in which the Legal Statute of the Catholic Church in Brazil is recognized.[^cite_note-442][^cite_note-443]

### Education

Main article: [Education in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Brazil)



Historical building of the [Federal University of Paraná](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_University_of_Paran%C3%A1), one of the oldest universities in Brazil, located in [Curitiba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curitiba)

The Federal Constitution and the Law of Guidelines and Bases of National Education determine that the union, the states, the Federal District and the municipalities must manage and organize their respective education systems. Each of these public educational systems is responsible for its own maintenance, which manages funds as well as the mechanisms and funding sources. The constitution reserves 25% of the state budget and 18% of federal taxes and municipal taxes for education.[^cite_note-Usa2005-444]

According to the [IBGE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Institute_of_Geography_and_Statistics), the literacy rate was 93.4% in 2019, meaning that 11.3 million (6.6% of population) people are still illiterate in the country, with some states such as [Rio de Janeiro](229419.md) and [Santa Catarina](256305.md) reaching around 97% of literacy rate;[^cite_note-NN-445] functional illiteracy has reached 21.6% of the population.[^cite_note-Agency2010-446] Illiteracy is higher in the [Northeast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Region,_Brazil), where 13.87% of the population is illiterate, while the [South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Region,_Brazil), has 3.3% illiteracy.[^cite_note-Bank2001-447][^cite_note-NN-445]

Brazil's private institutions tend to be more exclusive and offer better quality education, so many high-income families send their children there. The result is a segregated educational system that reflects extreme income disparities and reinforces social inequality. However, efforts to change this are making impacts.[^cite_note-448] The [University of São Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_S%C3%A3o_Paulo) is often considered [the best in Brazil and Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_and_university_rankings).[^cite_note-449][^cite_note-450] Of the top 20 Latin American universities, eight are Brazilian; most are [public](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_university). Attending an institution of higher education is required by Law of Guidelines and Bases of Education. Kindergarten, elementary and medium education are required of all students.[^cite_note-451]

### Health

Main article: [Health in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Brazil)

See also: [Healthcare in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Brazil) and [Sistema Único de Saúde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sistema_%C3%9Anico_de_Sa%C3%BAde)



The [Clinical Hospital of Porto Alegre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospital_de_Cl%C3%ADnicas_de_Porto_Alegre) is academically linked to the [Federal University of Rio Grande do Sul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_University_of_Rio_Grande_do_Sul) and is part of the [SUS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sistema_%C3%9Anico_de_Sa%C3%BAde), the Brazilian [publicly funded health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Publicly_funded_health_care) system.

The Brazilian [public health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_health) system, the [Unified Health System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sistema_%C3%9Anico_de_Sa%C3%BAde) (*Sistema Único de Saúde* – SUS), is managed and provided by all levels of government,[^cite_note-ForgiaCouttolenc2008-452] being the largest system of this type in the world.[^cite_note-453] On the other hand, private healthcare systems play a complementary role.[^cite_note-Wolper2004-454] Public health services are universal and offered to all citizens of the country for free. However, the construction and maintenance of health centers and hospitals are financed by taxes, and the country spends about 9% of its GDP on expenditures in the area. In 2021, Brazil had 2.1 doctors and 2.5 hospital beds for every 1,000 inhabitants.[^cite_note-455][^cite_note-456]

Despite all the progress made since the creation of the [universal health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_health_care) system in 1988, there are still several public health issues in Brazil. In 2023, [infant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_infant_mortality_rate) (2.51%) and maternal mortality rates (197.3 deaths per 100,000 births) were still high.[^cite_note-Radar_social-457]

The number of deaths from noncommunicable diseases, such as [cardiovascular diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardiovascular_diseases) (151.7 deaths per 100,000 inhabitants) and [cancer](105219.md) (72.7 deaths per 100,000 inhabitants), also has a considerable impact on the health of the Brazilian population. Finally, external but preventable factors such as car accidents, violence and suicide caused 14.9% of all deaths in the country.[^cite_note-Radar_social-457] The Brazilian health system was ranked 125th among the 191 countries evaluated by the [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO) in 2000.[^cite_note-458]

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Brazil)



Parade of [Portela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portela_\(samba_school\)) [samba school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samba_school) at the [Rio Carnival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_Carnival), the largest [carnival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnival) in the world

[^cite_note-Guiness-459]

The core culture of Brazil is derived from [Portuguese culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Portugal), due to its strong colonial ties with the Portuguese Empire.[^cite_note-Meade2009-460] Among other influences, the Portuguese introduced the Portuguese language, Roman Catholicism and colonial architectural styles. Brazilian culture was also strongly influenced by African, indigenous and non-Portuguese European cultures and traditions.[^cite_note-Levinson1998-461]

Some aspects of Brazilian culture were shaped by the contributions of successive waves of immigrants who arrived in large numbers in the south and southeast of Brazil during the 19th and 20th centuries, including Italians, Germans and other Europeans as well as Japanese, Jewish and Arab communities.[^cite_note-Lesser2013a-462] The indigenous Amerindians influenced Brazil's language and [cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_cuisine); and the Africans influenced language, cuisine, [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Brazil), dance and religion.[^cite_note-463]

[Brazilian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_art) has developed since the 16th century into different styles that range from [Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque_in_Brazil) (the dominant style in Brazil until the early 19th century)[^cite_note-464][^cite_note-itaucultural.org.br-465] to [Romanticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_academic_art), [Modernism](163543.md), [Expressionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expressionism), [Cubism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cubism), [Surrealism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surrealism) and [Abstractionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abstract_art). [Brazilian cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Brazil) dates back to the birth of the medium in the late 19th century and has gained a new level of international acclaim since the 1960s.[^cite_note-Marsh2012-466]

Brazilian culture has long enjoyed periodic [global influence and interest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soft_power), often described as the phenomenon of "Brazilianness" (*brasilidade*)—a unique blend of music, dance, cinema, sport and other cultural elements that attract emulation, admiration, and commentary.[^cite_note-467]

### Architecture

Main article: [Architecture of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Brazil)



The [Cathedral of Brasília](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_of_Bras%C3%ADlia), designed by Brazilian architect Oscar Niemeyer for the federal capital, an example of Modern architecture

The architecture of Brazil is influenced by Europe, especially Portugal. It has a history that goes back 500 years to the time, when Pedro Álvares Cabral landed in Brazil in 1500. [Portuguese colonial architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_colonial_architecture) was the first wave of architecture to go to Brazil.[^cite_note-468] It is the basis for all Brazilian architecture of later centuries.[^cite_note-469] In the 19th century, during the time of the Empire of Brazil, the country followed European trends and adopted [Neoclassical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassical_architecture) and [Gothic Revival architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_Revival_architecture). Then, in the 20th century, especially in Brasília, Brazil experimented with [modernist architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernist_architecture).

The colonial architecture of Brazil dates to the early 16th century, when Brazil was first explored, conquered and settled by the Portuguese. The Portuguese built architecture familiar to them in Europe in their aim to colonize Brazil. They built Portuguese colonial architecture, which included churches and civic architecture, including houses and forts, in Brazilian cities and the countryside.[^cite_note-Guimaraens-470]

During the 19th century, Brazilian architecture saw the introduction of more European styles to Brazil, such as Neoclassical and Gothic Revival architecture. This was usually mixed with Brazilian influences from their own heritage.[^cite_note-Guimaraens-470] In the 1950s modernist architecture was introduced when [Brasília](4752.md) was built as a new federal capital in the interior of Brazil to help develop the interior. The architect [Oscar Niemeyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oscar_Niemeyer) idealized and built government buildings, churches and civic buildings in the modernist style.[^cite_note-Claro-471]

### Visual arts

Main article: [Brazilian painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_painting)



*Entry in the Forest* mural at the [Thomas Jefferson Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson_Building) by Candido Portinari, one of the most important Brazilian painters

Brazilian painting emerged in the late 16th century,[^cite_note-472] influenced by [Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque), [Rococo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rococo), [Neoclassicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassicism), [Romanticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism), [Realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Realism_\(arts\)), [Modernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernism), [Expressionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expressionism), [Surrealism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surrealism), [Cubism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cubism) and [Abstracionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abstract_art) making it a major art style called [Brazilian academic art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_academic_art).[^cite_note-473][^cite_note-Biscardi-474]

The [French Artistic Mission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miss%C3%A3o_Art%C3%ADstica_Francesa) arrived in Brazil in 1816 proposing the creation of an art academy modeled after the respected Académie des Beaux-Arts, with graduation courses both for artists and craftsmen for activities such as modeling, decorating, carpentry and others and bringing artists such as [Jean-Baptiste Debret](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Baptiste_Debret).[^cite_note-Biscardi-474]

Upon the creation of the [Imperial Academy of Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Academy_of_Fine_Arts_\(Brazil\)), new artistic movements spread across the country during the 19th century and later the event called Modern Art Week broke with academic tradition in 1922 and started a nationalist trend which was influenced by modernist arts.[^cite_note-Amaral-475]

Among the best-known Brazilian painters are [Ricardo do Pilar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricardo_do_Pilar) and [Manoel da Costa Ataíde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manoel_da_Costa_Ata%C3%ADde) (baroque and rococo), [Victor Meirelles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Meirelles), [Pedro Américo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Am%C3%A9rico) and [Almeida Júnior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almeida_J%C3%BAnior) (romanticism and realism), [Anita Malfatti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anita_Malfatti), [Ismael Nery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismael_Nery), [Lasar Segall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lasar_Segall), [Emiliano Di Cavalcanti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emiliano_Di_Cavalcanti), [Vicente do Rego Monteiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicente_do_Rego_Monteiro), and [Tarsila do Amaral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarsila_do_Amaral) (expressionism, surrealism and cubism), [Aldo Bonadei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aldo_Bonadei), [José Pancetti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Pancetti) and [Candido Portinari](430593.md) (modernism).[^cite_note-476]

### Music

Main article: [Music of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Brazil)



[Tom Jobim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Jobim), one of the creators of *[bossa nova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bossa_nova)*, and [Chico Buarque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chico_Buarque), one of the leading names of [MPB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%BAsica_popular_brasileira)

The music of Brazil was formed mainly from the fusion of European, Native Indigenous, and African elements.[^cite_note-FonsecaWeiner1991-477] Until the nineteenth century, Portugal was the gateway to most of the influences that built Brazilian music, although many of these elements were not of Portuguese origin, but generally European. The first was [José Maurício Nunes Garcia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Maur%C3%ADcio_Nunes_Garcia), author of sacred pieces with an influence of Viennese classicism.[^cite_note-Grazia2013-478] The major contribution of the African element was the rhythmic diversity and some dances and instruments.[^cite_note-FonsecaWeiner1991-477]

Popular music since the late eighteenth century, [samba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samba) was considered the most typical and on the UNESCO cultural heritage list.[^cite_note-479] [Samba-reggae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samba_reggae), [Axé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ax%C3%A9), [Maracatu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maracatu), [Frevo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frevo) and [Afoxê](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afox%C3%AA) are four music traditions that have been popularized by their appearance in the annual [Brazilian Carnivals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Carnival).[^cite_note-Crook2009-480] [Capoeira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capoeira) is usually played with its own music referred to as [capoeira music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capoeira_music), which is usually considered to be a call-and-response type of folk music.[^cite_note-Fryer2000-481] [Forró](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forr%C3%B3) is a type of folk music prominent during the [Festa Junina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Festa_Junina) in [northeastern Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Region,_Brazil).[^cite_note-482] Jack A. Draper III, a professor of Portuguese at the [University of Missouri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Missouri),[^cite_note-483] argues that Forró was used as a way to subdue feelings of nostalgia for a rural lifestyle.[^cite_note-484]

[Choro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Choro) is a popular musical instrumental style. Its origins are in 19th-century Rio de Janeiro. The style often has a fast and happy rhythm, characterized by subtle [modulations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modulation_\(music\)) and full of [syncopation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syncopation) and [counterpoint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counterpoint).[^cite_note-MacGowanPessanha1998-485] [lambada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lambada_\(dance\)) and [Carimbó](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carimb%C3%B3) achieved success in Latin music, originating from Pará.[^cite_note-486] [Bossa nova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bossa_nova) is also a well-known style of Brazilian music developed and popularized in the 1950s and 1960s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacGowanPessanha1998\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid7MFD-EoTR7MCpgPA6_6\]-487] The phrase "bossa nova" means literally 'new trend'.[^cite_note-Kassing2007-488] A lyrical fusion of samba and [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz), bossa nova acquired a large following starting in the 1960s.[^cite_note-Campbell2011b-489]

### Literature

Main article: [Brazilian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_literature)



[Machado de Assis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machado_de_Assis) (1839–1908), poet and novelist, founder of the [Brazilian Academy of Letters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Academy_of_Letters), is associated with [romanticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism) and [literary realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literary_realism).



[Clarice Lispector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarice_Lispector) (1920–1977), novelist and short story writer, whose work engages themes of intimacy and introspection

[Brazilian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_literature) dates back to the 16th century, to the writings of the first Portuguese explorers in Brazil, such as [Pero Vaz de Caminha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pero_Vaz_de_Caminha), filled with descriptions of fauna, flora and commentary about the indigenous population that fascinated European readers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrocittiVallance2012\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidVhkvhllLooUCpgPA360_360\]-490]

Brazil produced significant works in [Romanticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism)—novelists such as [Joaquim Manuel de Macedo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joaquim_Manuel_de_Macedo) and [José de Alencar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_de_Alencar) wrote novels about love and pain. Alencar, in his long career, also treated indigenous people as heroes in the [Indigenist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indianism_\(arts\)) novels *[O Guarani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guarani)*, *[Iracema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iracema)* and *[Ubirajara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ubirajara_\(novel\))*.[^cite_note-491] [Machado de Assis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machado_de_Assis), one of his contemporaries, wrote in virtually all genres and continues to gain international prestige from critics worldwide.[^cite_note-492][^cite_note-493][^cite_note-494]

[Brazilian Modernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_literature#Modernism), evidenced by the [Modern Art Week](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Art_Week) in 1922, was concerned with a nationalist avant-garde literature,[^cite_note-495] while [Post-Modernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_literature#Post-Modernism) brought a generation of distinct poets such as [João Cabral de Melo Neto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jo%C3%A3o_Cabral_de_Melo_Neto), [Carlos Drummond de Andrade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Drummond_de_Andrade), [Vinicius de Moraes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vinicius_de_Moraes), [Cora Coralina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cora_Coralina), [Graciliano Ramos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graciliano_Ramos), [Cecília Meireles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cec%C3%ADlia_Meireles), and internationally known writers dealing with universal and regional subjects such as [Jorge Amado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Amado), [João Guimarães Rosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jo%C3%A3o_Guimar%C3%A3es_Rosa), [Clarice Lispector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarice_Lispector) and [Manuel Bandeira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Bandeira).[^cite_note-496][^cite_note-497][^cite_note-498]

Brazil's most significant literary award is the [Camões Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cam%C3%B5es_Prize), which it shares with the rest of the Portuguese-speaking world. As of 2016, Brazil has eleven recipients of the prize.[^cite_note-499] Brazil also holds its own literary academy, the [Brazilian Academy of Letters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Academy_of_Letters), a non-profit cultural organization aimed at perpetuating the care of the national language and literature.[^cite_note-500]

### Theatre



[Augusto Boal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augusto_Boal) presenting a workshop on the [Theatre of the Oppressed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theatre_of_the_Oppressed) at [Riverside Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riverside_Church) in [New York City](645042.md) in 2008

The theatre in Brazil has its origins in the period of [Jesuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesuits) expansion, when theater was used for the dissemination of Catholic doctrine in the 16th century. In the 17th and 18th centuries, dramatists on the scene of European derivation were for court or private performances.[^cite_note-501] During the 19th century, the playwrights [Antônio Gonçalves Dias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gon%C3%A7alves_Dias) and [Luís Carlos Martins Pena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martins_Pena) were known for their performance.[^cite_note-teatro-502] There were also numerous operas and orchestras. The Brazilian conductor [Antônio Carlos Gomes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ant%C3%B4nio_Carlos_Gomes) became internationally known with operas such as *[Il Guarany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Il_Guarany)*. At the end of the 19th century, orchestrated dramaturgias were accompanied with songs of famous artists such as the conductress [Chiquinha Gonzaga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiquinha_Gonzaga).[^cite_note-503]

Already in the early 20th century there was the presence of theaters, entrepreneurs and actor companies. In 1940, Paschoal Carlos Magno and his student's theater, the comedians group and the Italian actors [Adolfo Celi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolfo_Celi), Ruggero Jacobbi and Aldo Calvo, founders of the *Teatro Brasileiro de Comédia*, renewed the Brazilian theater. From the 1960s, it was attended by a theater dedicated to social and religious issues. The most prominent authors at this stage were [Jorge Andrade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Andrade_\(playwright\)) and [Ariano Suassuna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariano_Suassuna).[^cite_note-teatro-502]

### Cinema

Main article: [Cinema of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Brazil)



[Gramado Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Festival_de_Gramado), the biggest film festival in the country

The Brazilian film industry began in the late 19th century, during the early days of the [Belle Époque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belle_%C3%89poque). While there were national film productions during the early 20th century, American films such as *Rio the Magnificent* were made in Rio de Janeiro to promote tourism in the city.[^cite_note-504] The films *[Limite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limite)* (1931) and *[Ganga Bruta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganga_Bruta)* (1933), the latter being produced by [Adhemar Gonzaga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adhemar_Gonzaga) through the prolific studio Cinédia, were poorly received at release and failed at the box office, but are acclaimed nowadays and placed among the finest Brazilian films of all time.[^cite_note-505] The 1941 unfinished film *[It's All True](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/It's_All_True_\(film\))* was divided into four segments, two of which were filmed in Brazil and directed by [Orson Welles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orson_Welles); it was originally produced as part of the United States' [Good Neighbor Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_Neighbor_Policy) during Getúlio Vargas' Estado Novo.

During the 1960s, the [Cinema Novo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_Novo) movement rose to prominence with directors such as [Glauber Rocha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glauber_Rocha), [Nelson Pereira dos Santos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nelson_Pereira_dos_Santos), [Paulo César Saraceni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paulo_C%C3%A9sar_Saraceni) and [Arnaldo Jabor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnaldo_Jabor). Glauber Rocha's films *[Black God, White Devil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_God,_White_Devil)* (1964) and *[Entranced Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entranced_Earth)* (1967) are considered to be some of the greatest and most influential in Brazilian film history.[^cite_note-506] Rocha won the [Best Director Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Best_Director_Award_\(Cannes_Film_Festival\)) at the [1969 Cannes Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1969_Cannes_Film_Festival) for *[Antonio das Mortes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_das_Mortes)* and the [1977](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1977_Cannes_Film_Festival) [Special Jury Prize for Best Short Film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Short_Film_Palme_d'Or) for *Di*.

During the 1990s, Brazil saw a surge of critical and commercial success with films such as *[O Quatrilho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O_Quatrilho)* ([Fábio Barreto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%A1bio_Barreto), 1995), *[O Que É Isso, Companheiro?](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O_Que_%C3%89_Isso,_Companheiro%3F)* ([Bruno Barreto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruno_Barreto), 1997) and *[Central do Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Station_\(film\))* ([Walter Salles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Salles), 1998), all of which were nominated for the [Academy Award for Best Foreign Language Film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Foreign_Language_Film), the latter receiving a [Best Actress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Actress) nomination for [Fernanda Montenegro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernanda_Montenegro). The 2002 crime film *[City of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_God_\(2002_film\))*, directed by [Fernando Meirelles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Meirelles), was critically acclaimed, scoring 90% on [Rotten Tomatoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotten_Tomatoes),[^cite_note-507] being placed in [Roger Ebert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Ebert)'s Best Films of the Decade list[^cite_note-508] and receiving four [Academy Award](324.md) nominations in 2004, including [Best Director](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Director). Notable film festivals in Brazil include the [São Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Paulo_International_Film_Festival) and [Rio de Janeiro International Film Festivals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_International_Film_Festival) and the [Gramado Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Festival_de_Gramado).

The film [*I'm Still Here*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I'm_Still_Here_\(2024_film\)), directed by Walter Salles, was nominated for [Best Actress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Actress) (Torres) and [Best Picture](61702.md) at the [97th Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/97th_Academy_Awards),[^cite_note-509][^cite_note-510][^cite_note-511] and won [Best International Feature Film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_International_Feature_Film), becoming the first-ever Brazilian produced film to win an Academy Award.[^cite_note-512] The film [*The Secret Agent*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Secret_Agent_\(2025_film\)) had its world premiere at the main competition of the [2025 Cannes Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Cannes_Film_Festival) on 18 May 2025, where it won the [Best Actor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannes_Film_Festival_Award_for_Best_Actor) prize for [Wagner Moura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wagner_Moura), the [Best Director](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannes_Film_Festival_Award_for_Best_Director) prize for Mendonça and the [FIPRESCI Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Federation_of_Film_Critics). It received further widespread critical acclaim.[^cite_note-513]

### Media

Main articles: [Telecommunications in Brazil](3635.md) and [Television in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_in_Brazil)

See also: [Concentration of media ownership § Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concentration_of_media_ownership#Brazil), and [Mass media regulation in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media_regulation_in_Brazil)



Former President [Dilma Rousseff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dilma_Rousseff) at *[Jornal Nacional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jornal_Nacional)* news program. [Rede Globo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rede_Globo) is the world's second-largest commercial television network.

[^cite_note-514]

The Brazilian press was officially born in Rio de Janeiro on 13 May 1808 with the creation of the Royal Printing National Press by the Prince Regent John.[^cite_note-EchevarríaPupo-Walker1996-515] The *Gazeta do Rio de Janeiro*, the first newspaper published in the country, began to circulate on 10 September 1808.[^cite_note-Johnston2003-516] The largest newspapers nowadays are *[Folha de S.Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folha_de_S.Paulo)*, *[O Globo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O_Globo)*, and *[O Estado de S. Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O_Estado_de_S._Paulo)*.[^cite_note-Vincent2003B-517]

Radio broadcasting began on 7 September 1922, with a speech by then President [Epitácio Pessoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epit%C3%A1cio_Pessoa), and was formalized on 20 April 1923 with the creation of the "Radio Society of Rio de Janeiro".[^cite_note-McCann2004-518] Television in Brazil began officially on 18 September 1950, with the founding of [TV Tupi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TV_Tupi) by [Assis Chateaubriand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assis_Chateaubriand).[^cite_note-Ward2007-519] Since then, television has grown in the country, creating large commercial broadcast networks such as [Globo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rede_Globo), [SBT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sistema_Brasileiro_de_Televis%C3%A3o), [RecordTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RecordTV), [Bandeirantes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rede_Bandeirantes) and [RedeTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RedeTV!).

By the mid-1960s, Brazilian universities had installed [mainframe computers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainframe_computer) from [IBM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IBM) and [Burroughs Large Systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burroughs_Large_Systems). In the 1970s and 1980s, the Brazilian government restricted foreign imports to protect the local manufacturing of computers. In the 1980s, Brazil produced half of the computers sold in the country. By 2009, the mobile phone and Internet use in Brazil was the fifth largest in the world.[^cite_note-520] As of early 2025, internet penetration in Brazil stood at 86.2% of the total population (183 million individuals), and the ratio of cellular mobile connections to the total population was 102% (217 million active connections).[^cite_note-521]

In May 2010, the Brazilian government launched [TV Brasil Internacional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TV_Brasil_Internacional), an international television station, initially broadcasting to 49 countries.[^cite_note-522] Commercial television channels broadcast internationally include [Globo Internacional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globo_Internacional), [RecordTV Internacional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RecordTV_Internacional) and [Band Internacional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Band_Internacional).

### Cuisine

Main article: [Brazilian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_cuisine)

See also: [List of Brazilian dishes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Brazilian_dishes)



[Feijoada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feijoada_\(Brazilian_dish\)) is the national dish of Brazilian cuisine.

Brazilian cuisine varies greatly by region, reflecting the country's varying mix of indigenous and immigrant populations. This has created a national cuisine marked by the preservation of regional differences.[^cite_note-Encarta_4-523] Some of the most well known Brazilian foods are the [feijoada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feijoada_\(Brazilian_dish\)), considered the country's national dish;[^cite_note-524] and [churrasco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Churrasco), a kind of barbecue which is often served in [rodízio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rod%C3%ADzio) style. Other regional foods include beijú, [feijão tropeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feij%C3%A3o_tropeiro), [vatapá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vatap%C3%A1), [moqueca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moqueca), [polenta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polenta) (from Italian cuisine) and [acarajé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acaraj%C3%A9) (from African cuisine).[^cite_note-525] The national beverage is [coffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coffee); [cachaça](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cacha%C3%A7a) is Brazil's native liquor. Cachaça is distilled from sugar cane and is the main ingredient in the national cocktail, [Caipirinha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caipirinha).[^cite_note-Bayor2011-526]

A typical meal consists mostly of rice and beans with beef, salad, french fries and a fried egg.[^cite_note-527] Often, it is mixed with cassava flour ([farofa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farofa)). Fried potatoes, fried cassava, fried banana, fried meat and fried cheese are very often eaten in lunch and served in most typical restaurants.[^cite_note-528] Popular snacks are [pastel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pastel_\(Brazilian_food\)) (a fried pastry); [coxinha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coxinha) (a variation of chicken croquete); [pão de queijo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C3%A3o_de_queijo) (cheese bread and cassava flour / [tapioca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tapioca)); [pamonha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pamonha) (corn and milk paste); [esfirra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sfiha) (a variation of Lebanese pastry); [kibbeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kibbeh) (from Arabic cuisine); and empada (pastry), little salt pies filled with shrimps or heart of palm.

Brazil has a variety of desserts such as [brigadeiros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brigadeiro) (chocolate fudge balls), [bolo de rolo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolo_de_rolo) (roll cake with [goiabada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goiabada)), [cocada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cocada) (a coconut sweet), [beijinhos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beijinho) (coconut truffles and clove) and [Romeu e Julieta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romeu_e_Julieta) (cheese with goiabada). Peanuts are used to make [paçoca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pa%C3%A7oca), [rapadura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapadura) and [pé de moleque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C3%A9_de_moleque). Local common fruits such as [açaí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A%C3%A7a%C3%AD), [cupuaçu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cupua%C3%A7u), [mango](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mango), [papaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papaya), [cocoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cocoa_bean), [cashew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cashew), [guava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guava), [orange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_\(fruit\)), [lime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lime_\(fruit\)), [passionfruit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passionfruit), [pineapple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pineapple), and [hog plum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spondias) are turned into juices and used to make chocolates, ice pops and ice cream.[^cite_note-529]

### Sports

Main article: [Sport in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Brazil)

See also: [Brazil at the Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazil_at_the_Olympics) and [Brazil at the Paralympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazil_at_the_Paralympics)



[Pelé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pel%C3%A9), a [footballer](10669.md) as well as sport figure of the 20th century

[^cite_note-fifa-530]

[^cite_note-531]



[Ayrton Senna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayrton_Senna), the driver with [most consecutive wins](13599687.md#Most_consecutive_wins_at_the_same_Grand_Prix) and [pole positions](13599687.md#Most_consecutive_pole_positions_at_the_same_Grand_Prix) at the same [Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Formula_One_Grands_Prix) of [Formula One](10854.md)

The most popular sport in Brazil is [football](10568.md).[^cite_note-532] The [Brazilian men's national team](149286.md) is ranked among the best in the world according to the [FIFA World Rankings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIFA_World_Rankings), and has won the [World Cup](11370.md) tournament a record five times.[^cite_note-533][^cite_note-534]

[Volleyball](32558.md), [basketball](3921.md), [auto racing](1022.md) and [martial arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_arts) also have large audiences. The [Brazil men's national volleyball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazil_men's_national_volleyball_team) currently holds the titles of the [World League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIVB_Volleyball_World_League), [World Grand Champions Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIVB_Volleyball_World_Grand_Champions_Cup), [World Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIVB_Volleyball_Men's_World_Championship) and the [World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIVB_Volleyball_Men's_World_Cup). In auto racing, three Brazilian drivers have won the [Formula One](10854.md) world championship eight times.[^cite_note-535][^cite_note-536][^cite_note-537] The country has also produced significant achievements in other sports such as [sailing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sailing_\(sport\)), [swimming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swimming_\(sport\)), [tennis](29773.md), [surfing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surfing), [skateboarding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skateboarding), [MMA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MMA), [gymnastics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gymnastics), [boxing](4243.md), [judo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judo), [athletics](194904.md) and [table tennis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Table_tennis).

Some sport variations have their origins in Brazil: [beach football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beach_soccer),[^cite_note-538] [futsal](188197.md) (indoor football)[^cite_note-539] and [footvolley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Footvolley) emerged in Brazil as variations of football. In martial arts, Brazilians developed [Capoeira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capoeira),[^cite_note-540] [Vale tudo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vale_tudo)[^cite_note-541] and [Brazilian jiu-jitsu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_jiu-jitsu).[^cite_note-542]

Brazil has hosted several high-profile international sporting events, such as the [1950 FIFA World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1950_FIFA_World_Cup),[^cite_note-543] and recently has hosted the [2014 FIFA World Cup](656933.md), [2019 Copa América](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Copa_Am%C3%A9rica) and [2021 Copa América](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Copa_Am%C3%A9rica).[^cite_note-544] The São Paulo circuit, [Autódromo José Carlos Pace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aut%C3%B3dromo_Jos%C3%A9_Carlos_Pace), hosts the annual [Grand Prix of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Grand_Prix).[^cite_note-545] São Paulo organized the [IV Pan American Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1963_Pan_American_Games) in 1963, and Rio de Janeiro hosted the [XV Pan American Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Pan_American_Games) in 2007.[^cite_note-LiMacIntosh2011-546] On 2 October 2009, Rio de Janeiro was selected to host the [2016 Olympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Summer_Olympics) and [2016 Paralympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Summer_Paralympics), making it the first South American city to host the games[^cite_note-guardian_olympics-547] and second in Latin America, after [Mexico City](18987.md). Furthermore, the country hosted the [FIBA Basketball World Cups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIBA_Basketball_World_Cup) in [1954](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_FIBA_World_Championship) and [1963](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1963_FIBA_World_Championship). At the 1963 event, the [Brazil national basketball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazil_national_basketball_team) won one of its two world championship titles.[^cite_note-FIBA_History-548]

Nheengatú (also called língua geral of Amazonia, or lingua Brasilica) originated in the 17th century in what are now the states of Pará Maranhão, as lingua franca on the basis of Tupinambá lexicon but with strong grammatical influence from Portuguese, also due to intervention by Jesuit missionaries \[...] Around 1700 it was spoken in a large area in Brazil, as a contact language between whites and indians, but it lost some support with the expulsion of the Jesuits in 1758 \[...] Its sister language in the colonial period was Língua Geral Paulista (in the state of São Paulo) a lingua franca which is now extinct.
